<a href="https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/Category:_Manmohan_Singh" class="extiw" title="commons:Category: Manmohan Singh"><b>Gallery</b>: Picture, Sound, Video</a></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Manmohan_Singh_series" title="Template:Manmohan Singh series"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Manmohan_Singh_series" title="Template talk:Manmohan Singh series"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Manmohan_Singh_series&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Manmohan Singh</b> (<small>Punjabi:&#160;</small><span class="IPA nowrap" lang="pa-Latn-fonipa" title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Punjabi" title="Help:IPA/Punjabi">[mənˈmoːɦən<span class="wrap"> </span>ˈsɪ́ŋɡ]</a></span>&#32;<span data-nosnippet="" id="ooui-php-1" class="ext-phonos ext-phonos-PhonosButton noexcerpt ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel oo-ui-widget oo-ui-widget-enabled oo-ui-buttonElement oo-ui-buttonElement-frameless oo-ui-iconElement oo-ui-buttonWidget" data-ooui="{&quot;_&quot;:&quot;mw.Phonos.PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;\/\/upload.wikimedia.org\/wikipedia\/commons\/transcoded\/f\/f2\/Hi-ManmohanSingh.ogg\/Hi-ManmohanSingh.ogg.mp3&quot;,&quot;rel&quot;:[&quot;nofollow&quot;],&quot;framed&quot;:false,&quot;icon&quot;:&quot;volumeUp&quot;,&quot;data&quot;:{&quot;ipa&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;text&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;en&quot;,&quot;wikibase&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;file&quot;:&quot;Hi-ManmohanSingh.ogg&quot;},&quot;classes&quot;:[&quot;ext-phonos&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;noexcerpt&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel&quot;]}"><a role="button" tabindex="0" href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/f/f2/Hi-ManmohanSingh.ogg/Hi-ManmohanSingh.ogg.mp3" rel="nofollow" aria-label="Play audio" class="oo-ui-buttonElement-button"><span class="oo-ui-iconElement-icon oo-ui-icon-volumeUp"></span><span class="oo-ui-labelElement-label"></span><span class="oo-ui-indicatorElement-indicator oo-ui-indicatorElement-noIndicator"></span></a></span><sup class="ext-phonos-attribution noexcerpt"><a href="/wiki/File:Hi-ManmohanSingh.ogg" title="File:Hi-ManmohanSingh.ogg">i</a></sup>; born 26 September 1932) is an Indian politician, economist, academician and bureaucrat who served as the 13th <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_India" title="Prime Minister of India">Prime Minister of India</a> from 2004 to 2014. He is the third longest-serving prime minister after <a href="/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru" title="Jawaharlal Nehru">Jawaharlal Nehru</a> and <a href="/wiki/Indira_Gandhi" title="Indira Gandhi">Indira Gandhi</a>. A member of the <a href="/wiki/Indian_National_Congress" title="Indian National Congress">Indian National Congress</a>, Singh was the first <a href="/wiki/Sikh" class="mw-redirect" title="Sikh">Sikh</a> and non-Hindu prime minister of India. He was also the first prime minister since <a href="/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru" title="Jawaharlal Nehru">Jawaharlal Nehru</a> to be re-elected after completing a full five-year term.
</p><p>Born in <a href="/wiki/Gah,_Pakistan" title="Gah, Pakistan">Gah</a>, <a href="/wiki/Punjab_(region)" class="mw-redirect" title="Punjab (region)">West Punjab</a>, in what is today <a href="/wiki/Pakistan" title="Pakistan">Pakistan</a>, Singh's family migrated to India during <a href="/wiki/Partition_of_India" title="Partition of India">its partition</a> in 1947. After obtaining his doctorate in economics from <a href="/wiki/Nuffield_College,_Oxford" title="Nuffield College, Oxford">Oxford</a>, Singh worked for the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">UN</a> during 1966–1969. He subsequently began his bureaucratic career when <a href="/wiki/Lalit_Narayan_Mishra" title="Lalit Narayan Mishra">Lalit Narayan Mishra</a> hired him as an advisor in the <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Commerce_and_Industry_(India)" title="Ministry of Commerce and Industry (India)">Ministry of Commerce and Industry</a>. During the 1970s and 1980s, Singh held several key posts in the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_India" title="Government of India">Government of India</a>, such as <a href="/wiki/Chief_Economic_Advisor_to_the_Government_of_India" title="Chief Economic Advisor to the Government of India">Chief Economic Advisor</a> (1972–1976), governor of the <a href="/wiki/Reserve_Bank_of_India" title="Reserve Bank of India">Reserve Bank</a> (1982–1985) and head of the <a href="/wiki/Planning_Commission_(India)" title="Planning Commission (India)">Planning Commission</a> (1985–1987).
</p><p>In 1991, as India faced a <a href="/wiki/1991_Indian_economic_crisis" title="1991 Indian economic crisis">severe economic crisis</a>, the newly elected prime minister, <a href="/wiki/P._V._Narasimha_Rao" title="P. V. Narasimha Rao">P. V. Narasimha Rao</a>, inducted the apolitical Singh into his cabinet as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_(India)" title="Minister of Finance (India)">finance minister</a>. Over the next few years, despite strong opposition, he carried out several structural reforms that <a href="/wiki/Economic_liberalisation_in_India" title="Economic liberalisation in India">liberalised India's economy</a>. Although these measures proved successful in averting the crisis, and enhanced Singh's reputation globally as a leading reform-minded economist, the incumbent Congress Party fared poorly in the <a href="/wiki/1996_Indian_general_election" title="1996 Indian general election">1996 general election</a>. Subsequently, Singh was <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(India)" title="Leader of the Opposition (India)">leader of the opposition</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Rajya_Sabha" title="Rajya Sabha">Rajya Sabha</a> (the upper house of the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_India" title="Parliament of India">Parliament of India</a>) during the <a href="/wiki/Atal_Bihari_Vajpayee" title="Atal Bihari Vajpayee">Atal Bihari Vajpayee</a> government of 1998–2004.
</p><p>In 2004, when the Congress-led <a href="/wiki/United_Progressive_Alliance" title="United Progressive Alliance">United Progressive Alliance</a> came to power, its chairperson <a href="/wiki/Sonia_Gandhi" title="Sonia Gandhi">Sonia Gandhi</a> unexpectedly relinquished the prime ministership to Singh. His <a href="/wiki/First_Manmohan_Singh_ministry" title="First Manmohan Singh ministry">first ministry</a> executed several key legislations and projects, including the <a href="/wiki/National_Rural_Health_Mission" class="mw-redirect" title="National Rural Health Mission">National Rural Health Mission</a>, <a href="/wiki/Unique_Identification_Authority_of_India" class="mw-redirect" title="Unique Identification Authority of India">Unique Identification Authority</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi_National_Rural_Employment_Guarantee_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Act">Rural Employment Guarantee</a> scheme and <a href="/wiki/Right_to_Information_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Right to Information Act">Right to Information Act</a>. In 2008, opposition to a historic <a href="/wiki/U.S.%E2%80%93India_Civil_Nuclear_Agreement" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S.–India Civil Nuclear Agreement">civil nuclear agreement with the United States</a> nearly caused Singh's government to fall after <a href="/wiki/Left_Front_(West_Bengal)" title="Left Front (West Bengal)">Left Front</a> parties withdrew their support. India's economy grew rapidly under his reign.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/2009_Indian_general_election" title="2009 Indian general election">2009 general election</a> saw the UPA return with an increased mandate, with Singh retaining the office of Prime Minister. Over the next few years, Singh's <a href="/wiki/Second_Manmohan_Singh_ministry" title="Second Manmohan Singh ministry">second ministry</a> government faced a number of corruption charges over the organisation of the <a href="/wiki/2010_Commonwealth_Games" title="2010 Commonwealth Games">2010 Commonwealth Games</a>, the <a href="/wiki/2G_spectrum_allocation_case" class="mw-redirect" title="2G spectrum allocation case">2G spectrum allocation case</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Indian_coal_allocation_scam" title="Indian coal allocation scam">allocation of coal blocks</a>. After his term ended in 2014 he opted out from the race for the office of the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_India" title="Prime Minister of India">PM</a> during the <a href="/wiki/2014_Indian_general_election" title="2014 Indian general election">2014 Indian general election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Singh was never a <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament,_Lok_Sabha" title="Member of Parliament, Lok Sabha">member of the Lok Sabha</a> but has served as a member of the <a href="/wiki/Rajya_Sabha" title="Rajya Sabha">Rajya Sabha</a>, representing the state of <a href="/wiki/Assam" title="Assam">Assam</a> from 1991 to 2019 and <a href="/wiki/Rajasthan" title="Rajasthan">Rajasthan</a> since 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_education">Early life and education</span></h2>
<p>Singh was born to Gurmukh Singh and Amrit Kaur on 26 September 1932, in <a href="/wiki/Gah,_Pakistan" title="Gah, Pakistan">Gah</a>, <a href="/wiki/Punjab_(British_India)" class="mw-redirect" title="Punjab (British India)">Punjab</a>, <a href="/wiki/British_Raj" title="British Raj">British India</a>, into a <a href="/wiki/Sikh" class="mw-redirect" title="Sikh">Sikh</a> family.<sup id="cite_ref-dr_mms_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dr_mms-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> He lost his mother when he was very young and was raised by his paternal grandmother, to whom he was very close.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> His early schooling was in the <a href="/wiki/Urdu" title="Urdu">Urdu</a> medium, and even as Prime Minister years later, he wrote his apparently <a href="/wiki/Hindi" title="Hindi">Hindi</a> speeches in the <a href="/wiki/Urdu_script" class="mw-redirect" title="Urdu script">Urdu script</a>, although sometimes he would also use <a href="/wiki/Gurmukhi" title="Gurmukhi">Gurmukhi</a>, a script used to write <a href="/wiki/Punjabi_language" title="Punjabi language">Punjabi</a>, his mother tongue.
</p><p>After the <a href="/wiki/Partition_of_India" title="Partition of India">Partition of India</a>, his family migrated to <a href="/wiki/Amritsar" title="Amritsar">Amritsar</a>, India, where he studied at Hindu College, Amritsar.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> He attended <a href="/wiki/Panjab_University" title="Panjab University">Panjab University</a>, then in <a href="/wiki/Hoshiarpur" title="Hoshiarpur">Hoshiarpur</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Punjab, studying Economics and got his bachelor's and master's degrees in 1952 and 1954, respectively, standing first throughout his academic career. He completed his Economics Tripos at <a href="/wiki/University_of_Cambridge" title="University of Cambridge">University of Cambridge</a> in 1957. He was a member of <a href="/wiki/St_John%27s_College,_Cambridge" title="St John&#39;s College, Cambridge">St John's College</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-CSIR_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CSIR-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In a 2005 interview with the British journalist <a href="/wiki/Mark_Tully" title="Mark Tully">Mark Tully</a>, Singh said about his Cambridge days:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I first became conscious of the creative role of politics in shaping human affairs, and I owe that mostly to my teachers <a href="/wiki/Joan_Robinson" title="Joan Robinson">Joan Robinson</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_Kaldor" title="Nicholas Kaldor">Nicholas Kaldor</a>. Joan Robinson was a brilliant teacher, but she also sought to awaken the inner conscience of her students in a manner that very few others were able to achieve. She questioned me a great deal and made me think the unthinkable. She propounded the left wing interpretation of Keynes, maintaining that the state has to play more of a role if you really want to combine development with social equity. Kaldor influenced me even more; I found him pragmatic, scintillating, stimulating. Joan Robinson was a great admirer of what was going on in China, but Kaldor used the Keynesian analysis to demonstrate that capitalism could be made to work.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>After Cambridge, Singh returned to India and served as a teacher at <a href="/wiki/Panjab_University" title="Panjab University">Panjab University</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-TULLY_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TULLY-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> In 1960, he went to the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Oxford" title="University of Oxford">University of Oxford</a> for his <a href="/wiki/DPhil" class="mw-redirect" title="DPhil">DPhil</a>, where he was a member of <a href="/wiki/Nuffield_College,_Oxford" title="Nuffield College, Oxford">Nuffield College</a>. His 1962 doctoral thesis under the supervision of <a href="/wiki/Ian_Little_(economist)" title="Ian Little (economist)">I.M.D. Little</a> was titled "India's export performance, 1951–1960, export prospects and policy implications", and was later the basis for his book "India's Export Trends and Prospects for Self-Sustained Growth".<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_career">Early career</span></h2>
<p>After completing his D.Phil., Singh returned to India. He was a senior lecturer of economics at <a href="/wiki/Panjab_University" title="Panjab University">Panjab University</a> from 1957 to 1959. During 1959 and 1963, he served as a <a href="/wiki/Reader_(academic_rank)" title="Reader (academic rank)">reader</a> in economics at Panjab University, and from 1963 to 1965, he was an economics professor there.<sup id="cite_ref-Bhushan_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bhushan-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Then he went to work for the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Conference_on_Trade_and_Development" title="United Nations Conference on Trade and Development">United Nations Conference on Trade and Development</a> (UNCTAD) from 1966 to 1969.<sup id="cite_ref-CSIR_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CSIR-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Later, he was appointed as an advisor to the Ministry of Foreign Trade by <a href="/wiki/Lalit_Narayan_Mishra" title="Lalit Narayan Mishra">Lalit Narayan Mishra</a>, in recognition of Singh's talent as an economist.<sup id="cite_ref-Advisor_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Advisor-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From 1969 to 1971, Singh was a professor of international trade at the <a href="/wiki/Delhi_School_of_Economics" title="Delhi School of Economics">Delhi School of Economics</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Delhi" class="mw-redirect" title="University of Delhi">University of Delhi</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-CSIR_8-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CSIR-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1972, Singh was chief economic adviser in the Ministry of Finance, and in 1976 he was secretary in the Finance Ministry.<sup id="cite_ref-CSIR_8-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CSIR-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> In 1980–1982 he was at the Planning Commission, and in 1982, he was appointed governor of the <a href="/wiki/Reserve_Bank_of_India" title="Reserve Bank of India">Reserve Bank of India</a> under then finance minister Pranab Mukherjee and held the post until 1985.<sup id="cite_ref-CSIR_8-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CSIR-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> He went on to become the deputy chairman of the <a href="/wiki/Planning_Commission_(India)" title="Planning Commission (India)">Planning Commission (India)</a> from 1985 to 1987.<sup id="cite_ref-dr_mms_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dr_mms-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Following his tenure at the Planning Commission, he was secretary general of the <a href="/wiki/South_Centre_(organization)" title="South Centre (organization)">South Commission</a>, an independent economic policy think tank headquartered in <a href="/wiki/Geneva" title="Geneva">Geneva</a>, Switzerland from 1987 to November 1990.<sup id="cite_ref-India_–_Head_of_Government_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-India_–_Head_of_Government-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Singh returned to India from Geneva in November 1990 and held the post as the advisor to Prime Minister of India on economic affairs during the tenure of <a href="/wiki/Chandra_Shekhar" title="Chandra Shekhar">Chandra Shekar</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-CSIR_8-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CSIR-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> In March 1991, he became chairman of the University Grants Commission.<sup id="cite_ref-CSIR_8-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CSIR-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Family_and_personal_life">Family and personal life</span></h2>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Prime_Minister_Singh_and_Smt._Kaur_in_New_Delhi_on_October_15,_2010.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Prime_Minister_Singh_and_Smt._Kaur_in_New_Delhi_on_October_15%2C_2010.jpg/221px-Prime_Minister_Singh_and_Smt._Kaur_in_New_Delhi_on_October_15%2C_2010.jpg" decoding="async" width="221" height="170" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Prime_Minister_Singh_and_Smt._Kaur_in_New_Delhi_on_October_15%2C_2010.jpg/332px-Prime_Minister_Singh_and_Smt._Kaur_in_New_Delhi_on_October_15%2C_2010.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Prime_Minister_Singh_and_Smt._Kaur_in_New_Delhi_on_October_15%2C_2010.jpg/442px-Prime_Minister_Singh_and_Smt._Kaur_in_New_Delhi_on_October_15%2C_2010.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1030" data-file-height="791" /></a><figcaption>Prime Minister Singh and his wife <a href="/wiki/Gursharan_Kaur" title="Gursharan Kaur">Gursharan Kaur</a> in 2010</figcaption></figure>
<p>Singh married <a href="/wiki/Gursharan_Kaur" title="Gursharan Kaur">Gursharan Kaur</a> in 1958. They have three daughters, <a href="/wiki/Upinder_Singh" title="Upinder Singh">Upinder Singh</a>, <a href="/wiki/Daman_Singh" title="Daman Singh">Daman Singh</a> and Amrit Singh.<sup id="cite_ref-personal_profile_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-personal_profile-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Upinder Singh is a professor of history at <a href="/wiki/Ashoka_University" title="Ashoka University">Ashoka University</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> She has written six books, including <i>Ancient Delhi</i> (1999) and <i>A History of Ancient and Early Medieval India</i> (2008). Daman Singh is a graduate of <a href="/wiki/St._Stephen%27s_College,_Delhi" title="St. Stephen&#39;s College, Delhi">St. Stephen's College, Delhi</a> and <a href="/wiki/Institute_of_Rural_Management,_Anand" class="mw-redirect" title="Institute of Rural Management, Anand">Institute of Rural Management</a>, Anand, Gujarat, and author of <i>The Last Frontier: People and Forests in Mizoram</i> and a novel <i>Nine by Nine</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Amrit Singh is a staff attorney at the <a href="/wiki/American_Civil_Liberties_Union" title="American Civil Liberties Union">American Civil Liberties Union</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Ashok Pattnaik, 1983 batch <a href="/wiki/Indian_Police_Service" title="Indian Police Service">Indian Police Service</a> officer, son-in-law of former Prime Minister Manmohan Singh, was appointed CEO of National Intelligence Grid (<a href="/wiki/NATGRID" title="NATGRID">NATGRID</a>) in 2016.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Singh has undergone multiple <a href="/wiki/Cardiac_bypass_surgery" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardiac bypass surgery">cardiac bypass surgeries</a>, the most recent of which took place in January 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_career">Political career</span></h2>
<p>In June 1991, India's prime minister at the time, <a href="/wiki/P._V._Narasimha_Rao" title="P. V. Narasimha Rao">P. V. Narasimha Rao</a>, chose Singh to be his finance minister. Singh told Mark Tully the British journalist in 2005 "On the day (Rao) was formulating his cabinet, he sent his Principal Secretary to me saying, 'The PM would like you to become the Minister of Finance'. I didn't take it seriously. He eventually tracked me down the next morning, rather angry, and demanded that I get dressed up and come to Rashtrapati Bhavan for the swearing in. So that's how I started in politics".<sup id="cite_ref-TULLY_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TULLY-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_of_Finance">Minister of Finance</span></h3>
<p>In 1991, India's fiscal deficit was close to 8.5 per cent of the gross domestic product, the <a href="/wiki/Balance_of_payments" title="Balance of payments">balance of payments</a> deficit was huge and the <a href="/wiki/Current_account_deficit" class="mw-redirect" title="Current account deficit">current account deficit</a> was close to 3.5 percent of India's GDP.<sup id="cite_ref-rediff_Business_Desk_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rediff_Business_Desk-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> India's foreign reserves barely amounted to US$1&#160;billion, enough to pay for 2 weeks of imports,<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> in comparison to US$600&#160;billion today.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Evidently, India was facing an economic crisis. At this point, the government of India sought funds from the supranational <a href="/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund" title="International Monetary Fund">International Monetary Fund</a>, which, while assisting India financially, imposed several conditions regarding India's economic policy. In effect, IMF-dictated policy meant that the ubiquitous <a href="/wiki/Licence_Raj" title="Licence Raj">Licence Raj</a> had to be dismantled, and India's attempt at a state-controlled economy had to end.
</p><p>Singh explained to the PM and the party that India is facing an unprecedented crisis.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> However the rank and file of the party resisted deregulation.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_23-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> So Chidambaram and Singh explained to the party that the economy would collapse if it was not deregulated.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_23-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> To the dismay of the party, Rao allowed Singh to deregulate the <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_India" title="Economy of India">Indian economy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_23-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Subsequently, Singh, who had thus far been one of the most influential architects of India's socialist economy, eliminated the permit raj,<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_23-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> reduced state control of the economy, and reduced import taxes<sup id="cite_ref-rediff_Business_Desk_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rediff_Business_Desk-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Rao and Singh thus implemented policies to open up the economy and change India's socialist economy to a more <a href="/wiki/Capitalism" title="Capitalism">capitalistic</a> one, in the process dismantling the Licence Raj, a system that inhibited the prosperity of private businesses. They removed many obstacles standing in the way of <a href="/wiki/Foreign_Direct_Investment" class="mw-redirect" title="Foreign Direct Investment">Foreign Direct Investment</a> (FDI), and initiated the process of the privatisation of <a href="/wiki/Public_sector" title="Public sector">public sector</a> companies. However, in spite of these reforms, Rao's government was voted out in 1996 due to non-performance of government in other areas. In praise of Singh's work that pushed India towards a market economy, long-time Cabinet minister <a href="/wiki/P._Chidambaram" title="P. Chidambaram">P. Chidambaram</a> has compared Singh's role in India's reforms to <a href="/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping" title="Deng Xiaoping">Deng Xiaoping</a>'s in <a href="/wiki/China" title="China">China</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1993, Singh offered his resignation from the post of Finance Minister after a parliamentary investigation report criticised his ministry for not being able to anticipate a US$1.8&#160;billion <a href="/wiki/1992_Indian_stock_market_scam" title="1992 Indian stock market scam">securities scandal</a>. Prime Minister Rao refused Singh's resignation, instead promising to punish the individuals directly accused in the report.<sup id="cite_ref-nytimes_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nytimes-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_Opposition_in_Rajya_Sabha">Leader of Opposition in Rajya Sabha</span></h3>
<p>Singh was first elected to the upper house of Parliament, the <a href="/wiki/Rajya_Sabha" title="Rajya Sabha">Rajya Sabha</a>, in 1991<sup id="cite_ref-MSRj01_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MSRj01-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Rajya_Sabha#Membership" title="Rajya Sabha">by the legislature</a> of the state of Assam, and was re-elected in 1995, 2001, 2007<sup id="cite_ref-dr_mms_4-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dr_mms-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> and 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> From 1998 to 2004, while the <a href="/wiki/Bharatiya_Janata_Party" title="Bharatiya Janata Party">Bharatiya Janata Party</a> was in power, Singh was the Leader of the Opposition in the Rajya Sabha. In 1999, he contested for the <a href="/wiki/Lok_Sabha" title="Lok Sabha">Lok Sabha</a> from <a href="/wiki/South_Delhi_(Lok_Sabha_constituency)" class="mw-redirect" title="South Delhi (Lok Sabha constituency)">South Delhi</a> but was unable to win the seat.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister">Prime Minister</span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1134653256">.mw-parser-output .side-box{margin:4px 0;box-sizing:border-box;border:1px solid #aaa;font-size:88%;line-height:1.25em;background-color:#f9f9f9;display:flow-root}.mw-parser-output .side-box-abovebelow,.mw-parser-output .side-box-text{padding:0.25em 0.9em}.mw-parser-output .side-box-image{padding:2px 0 2px 0.9em;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .side-box-imageright{padding:2px 0.9em 2px 0;text-align:center}@media(min-width:500px){.mw-parser-output .side-box-flex{display:flex;align-items:center}.mw-parser-output .side-box-text{flex:1}}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .side-box{width:238px}.mw-parser-output .side-box-right{clear:right;float:right;margin-left:1em}.mw-parser-output .side-box-left{margin-right:1em}}</style><div class="side-box side-box-right plainlinks sistersitebox"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1126788409">.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul{line-height:inherit;list-style:none;margin:0;padding:0}.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol li,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul li{margin-bottom:0}</style>
<div class="side-box-flex">
<div class="side-box-image"><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Wikinews-logo.svg/40px-Wikinews-logo.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="22" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Wikinews-logo.svg/60px-Wikinews-logo.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Wikinews-logo.svg/80px-Wikinews-logo.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="759" data-file-height="415" /></span></span></div>

</div>
<h3><span id="First_term:_2004.E2.80.932009"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="First_term:_2004–2009">First term: 2004–2009</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/First_Manmohan_Singh_ministry" title="First Manmohan Singh ministry">First Manmohan Singh ministry</a></div>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_President_Dr._A.P.J_Abdul_Kalam_authorizing_the_Prime_Minister_designate_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_to_form_the_next_Government_in_New_Delhi_on_May_19,_2004.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/The_President_Dr._A.P.J_Abdul_Kalam_authorizing_the_Prime_Minister_designate_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_to_form_the_next_Government_in_New_Delhi_on_May_19%2C_2004.jpg/250px-The_President_Dr._A.P.J_Abdul_Kalam_authorizing_the_Prime_Minister_designate_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_to_form_the_next_Government_in_New_Delhi_on_May_19%2C_2004.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="179" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/The_President_Dr._A.P.J_Abdul_Kalam_authorizing_the_Prime_Minister_designate_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_to_form_the_next_Government_in_New_Delhi_on_May_19%2C_2004.jpg/375px-The_President_Dr._A.P.J_Abdul_Kalam_authorizing_the_Prime_Minister_designate_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_to_form_the_next_Government_in_New_Delhi_on_May_19%2C_2004.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/The_President_Dr._A.P.J_Abdul_Kalam_authorizing_the_Prime_Minister_designate_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_to_form_the_next_Government_in_New_Delhi_on_May_19%2C_2004.jpg/500px-The_President_Dr._A.P.J_Abdul_Kalam_authorizing_the_Prime_Minister_designate_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_to_form_the_next_Government_in_New_Delhi_on_May_19%2C_2004.jpg 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="643" /></a><figcaption>11th <a href="/wiki/President_of_India" title="President of India">President of India</a> <a href="/wiki/A._P._J._Abdul_Kalam" title="A. P. J. Abdul Kalam">A. P. J. Abdul Kalam</a> authorizing the Prime Minister designate Manmohan Singh to form the next Government in New Delhi on 19 May 2004</figcaption></figure>
<p>After the <a href="/wiki/2004_Indian_general_election" title="2004 Indian general election">2004 general elections</a>, the Indian National Congress ended the incumbent <a href="/wiki/National_Democratic_Alliance_(India)" class="mw-redirect" title="National Democratic Alliance (India)">National Democratic Alliance</a> (NDA) tenure by becoming the political party with the single largest number of seats in the <a href="/wiki/Lok_Sabha" title="Lok Sabha">Lok Sabha</a>. It formed <a href="/wiki/United_Progressive_Alliance" title="United Progressive Alliance">United Progressive Alliance</a> (UPA) with allies and staked claim to form government. In a surprise move, Chairperson <a href="/wiki/Sonia_Gandhi" title="Sonia Gandhi">Sonia Gandhi</a> declared Manmohan Singh, a <a href="/wiki/Technocracy" title="Technocracy">technocrat</a>, as the UPA candidate for the Prime Ministership. Despite the fact that Singh had never won a Lok Sabha seat, according to the <a href="/wiki/BBC" title="BBC">BBC</a>, he "enjoyed massive popular support, not least because he was seen by many as a clean politician untouched by the taint of corruption that has run through many Indian administrations."<sup id="cite_ref-bbcprofile_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bbcprofile-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> He took the oath as the Prime Minister of India on 22 May 2004.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Economic_policy">Economic policy</span></h4>
<p>In 1991, Singh, as Finance Minister, abolished the <a href="/wiki/Licence_Raj" title="Licence Raj">Licence Raj</a>, source of slow economic growth and <a href="/wiki/Corruption_in_India" title="Corruption in India">corruption in the Indian economy</a> for decades. He liberalised the Indian economy, allowing it to speed up development dramatically. During his term as Prime Minister, Singh continued to encourage growth in the Indian market, enjoying widespread success in these matters. Singh, along with his Finance Minister, <a href="/wiki/P._Chidambaram" title="P. Chidambaram">P. Chidambaram</a>, presided over a period where the Indian economy grew with an 8–9% economic growth rate. In 2007, India achieved its highest GDP growth rate of 9% and became the second <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_real_GDP_growth_rate" title="List of countries by real GDP growth rate">fastest growing major economy</a> in the world.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-astaire_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-astaire-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Singh's ministry enacted a <a href="/wiki/National_Rural_Employment_Guarantee_Act,_2005" class="mw-redirect" title="National Rural Employment Guarantee Act, 2005">National Employment Guarantee Act</a> (MGNREGA) in 2005.
</p><p>Singh's government continued the <a href="/wiki/Golden_Quadrilateral" title="Golden Quadrilateral">Golden Quadrilateral</a> and the highway modernisation program that was initiated by <a href="/wiki/Atal_Bihari_Vajpayee" title="Atal Bihari Vajpayee">Vajpayee</a>'s government.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Singh also worked on reforming the banking and financial sectors, as well as public sector companies.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> The Finance ministry worked towards relieving farmers of their debt and worked towards pro-industry policies.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> In 2005, Singh's government introduced the <a href="/wiki/Value_added_tax" class="mw-redirect" title="Value added tax">value added tax</a>, replacing <a href="/wiki/Sales_tax" title="Sales tax">sales tax</a>. In 2007 and early 2008, the <a href="/wiki/Economic_crisis_of_2008#Inflation" class="mw-redirect" title="Economic crisis of 2008">global problem of inflation</a> impacted India.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Healthcare_and_education">Healthcare and education</span></h4>
<p>In 2005, Prime Minister Singh and his government's health ministry started the <a href="/wiki/National_Rural_Health_Mission" class="mw-redirect" title="National Rural Health Mission">National Rural Health Mission</a> (NHRM), which mobilised half a million community health workers. This rural health initiative was praised by the American economist <a href="/wiki/Jeffrey_Sachs" title="Jeffrey Sachs">Jeffrey Sachs</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-timepoverty_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-timepoverty-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In 2006, his Government implemented the proposal to reserve 27% of seats in All India Institute of Medical Studies (AIIMS), Indian Institutes of Technology (IITs), the Indian Institutes of Management (IIMs) and other central institutions of higher education for Other Backward Classes which led to <a href="/wiki/2006_Indian_anti-reservation_protests" title="2006 Indian anti-reservation protests">2006 Indian anti-reservation protests</a>.
</p><p>On 2 July 2009, Singh ministry introduced The <a href="/wiki/Right_of_Children_to_Free_and_Compulsory_Education_Act,_2009" title="Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act, 2009">Right to Education Act</a> (RTE) act. Eight <a href="/wiki/Indian_Institutes_of_Technology" title="Indian Institutes of Technology">IIT's</a> were opened in the states of <a href="/wiki/Andhra_Pradesh" title="Andhra Pradesh">Andhra Pradesh</a>, Bihar, <a href="/wiki/Gujarat" title="Gujarat">Gujarat</a>, Orissa, <a href="/wiki/Punjab,_India" title="Punjab, India">Punjab</a>, Madhya Pradesh, <a href="/wiki/Rajasthan" title="Rajasthan">Rajasthan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Himachal_Pradesh" title="Himachal Pradesh">Himachal Pradesh</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> The Singh government also continued the <a href="/wiki/Sarva_Shiksha_Abhiyan" title="Sarva Shiksha Abhiyan">Sarva Shiksha Abhiyan</a> programme. The programme includes the introduction and improvement of mid-day meals and the opening of schools all over India, especially in rural areas, to fight <a href="/wiki/Illiteracy" class="mw-redirect" title="Illiteracy">illiteracy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Security_and_Home_Affairs">Security and Home Affairs</span></h4>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{display:flex;flex-direction:row;clear:left;flex-wrap:wrap;width:100%;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{margin:1px;float:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .theader{clear:both;font-weight:bold;text-align:center;align-self:center;background-color:transparent;width:100%}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbcaption{background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-left{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-right{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-center{text-align:center}@media all and (max-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbinner{width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;max-width:none!important;align-items:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{justify-content:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{float:none!important;max-width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle .thumbcaption{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow>.thumbcaption{text-align:center}}</style>
<p>Singh's government strengthened anti-terror laws with amendments to <a href="/wiki/Unlawful_Activities_(Prevention)_Act" title="Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act">Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act</a> (UAPA). <a href="/wiki/National_Investigation_Agency_(India)" class="mw-redirect" title="National Investigation Agency (India)">National Investigation Agency</a> (NIA) was also created soon after the <a href="/wiki/2008_Mumbai_attacks" title="2008 Mumbai attacks">Nov 2008 Mumbai terror attacks</a>, as need for a central agency to combat terrorism was realised. Also, <a href="/wiki/Unique_Identification_Authority_of_India" class="mw-redirect" title="Unique Identification Authority of India">Unique Identification Authority of India</a> was established in February 2009, an agency responsible for implementing the envisioned <a href="/wiki/Multipurpose_National_Identity_Card" class="mw-redirect" title="Multipurpose National Identity Card">Multipurpose National Identity Card</a> with the objective of increasing national security and facilitating e-governance.
</p><p>Singh's administration initiated a massive reconstruction effort in <a href="/wiki/Kashmir" title="Kashmir">Kashmir</a> to stabilise the region but after some period of success, insurgent infiltration and terrorism in Kashmir has increased since 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-Buzz7_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Buzz7-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> However, the Singh administration was successful in reducing terrorism in Northeast India.<sup id="cite_ref-Buzz7_43-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Buzz7-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Notable_legislation">Notable legislation</span></h4>
<p>The important <a href="/wiki/National_Rural_Employment_Guarantee_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="National Rural Employment Guarantee Act">National Rural Employment Guarantee Act</a> (NREGA) and the <a href="/wiki/Right_to_Information_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Right to Information Act">Right to Information Act</a> were passed by the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_India" title="Parliament of India">Parliament</a> in 2005 during his tenure. While the effectiveness of the NREGA has been successful at various degrees, in various regions, the RTI act has proved crucial in India's fight against corruption.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> New cash benefits were also introduced for widows, pregnant women, and landless persons.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/The_Right_to_Fair_Compensation_and_Transparency_in_Land_Acquisition,_Rehabilitation_and_Resettlement_Act,_2013" class="mw-redirect" title="The Right to Fair Compensation and Transparency in Land Acquisition, Rehabilitation and Resettlement Act, 2013">The Right to Fair Compensation and Transparency in Land Acquisition, Rehabilitation and Resettlement Act, 2013</a> was passed on 29 August 2013 in the <a href="/wiki/Lok_Sabha" title="Lok Sabha">Lok Sabha</a> (lower house of the Indian parliament) and on 4 September 2013 in <a href="/wiki/Rajya_Sabha" title="Rajya Sabha">Rajya Sabha</a> (upper house of the Indian parliament). The bill received the assent of the <a href="/wiki/President_of_India" title="President of India">President of India</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pranab_Mukherjee" title="Pranab Mukherjee">Pranab Mukherjee</a> on 27 September 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-ndtv1_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ndtv1-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> The Act came into force from 1 January 2014.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Right_of_Children_to_Free_and_Compulsory_Education_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act">Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act</a> was enacted on 4 August 2009, which describes the modalities of the importance of free and compulsory education for children between 6 and 14 in India under Article 21A of the <a href="/wiki/Indian_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="Indian Constitution">Indian Constitution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> India became one of 135 countries to make <a href="/wiki/Right_to_education" title="Right to education">education a fundamental right</a> of every child when the act came into force on 1 April 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti"><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:204px;max-width:204px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:202px;max-width:202px"><div class="thumbimage"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Barack_Obama_with_Prime_Minister_Manmohan_Singh_2009-11-24(2).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Manmohan Singh with American President Barack Obama at the White House" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/President_Barack_Obama_with_Prime_Minister_Manmohan_Singh_2009-11-24%282%29.jpg/200px-President_Barack_Obama_with_Prime_Minister_Manmohan_Singh_2009-11-24%282%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="133" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/President_Barack_Obama_with_Prime_Minister_Manmohan_Singh_2009-11-24%282%29.jpg/300px-President_Barack_Obama_with_Prime_Minister_Manmohan_Singh_2009-11-24%282%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/President_Barack_Obama_with_Prime_Minister_Manmohan_Singh_2009-11-24%282%29.jpg/400px-President_Barack_Obama_with_Prime_Minister_Manmohan_Singh_2009-11-24%282%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4096" data-file-height="2731" /></a></span></div></div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:202px;max-width:202px"><div class="thumbimage"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Prime_Minister,_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_with_the_American_President,_Mr._George_W._Bush_interacting_with_media_at_oval_office,_in_Washington_DC,_during_his_visit_to_the_United_States,_on_September_25,_2008.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="The Prime Minister, Dr. Manmohan Singh with the American President, Mr. George W. Bush interacting with media at oval office, in Washington DC" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/The_Prime_Minister%2C_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_with_the_American_President%2C_Mr._George_W._Bush_interacting_with_media_at_oval_office%2C_in_Washington_DC%2C_during_his_visit_to_the_United_States%2C_on_September_25%2C_2008.jpg/200px-thumbnail.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="122" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/The_Prime_Minister%2C_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_with_the_American_President%2C_Mr._George_W._Bush_interacting_with_media_at_oval_office%2C_in_Washington_DC%2C_during_his_visit_to_the_United_States%2C_on_September_25%2C_2008.jpg/300px-thumbnail.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/The_Prime_Minister%2C_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_with_the_American_President%2C_Mr._George_W._Bush_interacting_with_media_at_oval_office%2C_in_Washington_DC%2C_during_his_visit_to_the_United_States%2C_on_September_25%2C_2008.jpg/400px-thumbnail.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2100" data-file-height="1281" /></a></span></div><div class="thumbcaption">Manmohan Singh with American President <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Barack Obama</a> and <a href="/wiki/George_W._Bush" title="George W. Bush">George W. Bush</a> at the <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a></div></div></div></div></div>
<p>Manmohan Singh continued the <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_India" title="Foreign relations of India">pragmatic foreign policy</a> that was started by <a href="/wiki/P.V._Narasimha_Rao" class="mw-redirect" title="P.V. Narasimha Rao">P.V. Narasimha Rao</a> and continued by <a href="/wiki/Bharatiya_Janata_Party" title="Bharatiya Janata Party">Bharatiya Janata Party</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Atal_Bihari_Vajpayee" title="Atal Bihari Vajpayee">Atal Bihari Vajpayee</a>. Singh continued the <a href="/wiki/India-Pakistan_relations" class="mw-redirect" title="India-Pakistan relations">peace process with Pakistan</a> initiated by his predecessor, Atal Bihari Vajpayee. Exchange of high-level visits by top leaders from both countries have highlighted his tenure. Efforts have been made during Singh's tenure to end the border dispute with People's Republic of China. In November 2006, Chinese President <a href="/wiki/Hu_Jintao" title="Hu Jintao">Hu Jintao</a> visited India which was followed by Singh's visit to Beijing in January 2008. A major development in <a href="/wiki/Sino-Indian_relations" class="mw-redirect" title="Sino-Indian relations">Sino-Indian relations</a> was the reopening of the <a href="/wiki/Nathula_Pass" class="mw-redirect" title="Nathula Pass">Nathula Pass</a> in 2006 after being closed for more than four decades.<sup id="cite_ref-state_visit_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-state_visit-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_the_People%27s_Republic_of_China" title="Premier of the People&#39;s Republic of China">Premier of the State Council</a> of the People's Republic of China, <a href="/wiki/Li_Keqiang" title="Li Keqiang">Li Keqiang</a> paid a state visit to India (Delhi-<a href="/wiki/Mumbai" title="Mumbai">Mumbai</a>) from 19 to 21 May 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-state_visit_55-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-state_visit-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Singh paid an official visit to China from 22 to 24 October 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-state_visit_55-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-state_visit-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Signed were three agreements establishing <a href="/wiki/Sister_Cities_International" title="Sister Cities International">sister-city partnership</a> between Delhi-<a href="/wiki/Beijing" title="Beijing">Beijing</a>, Kolkata-<a href="/wiki/Kunming" title="Kunming">Kunming</a> and Bangalore-<a href="/wiki/Chengdu" title="Chengdu">Chengdu</a>. As of 2010, the People's Republic of China is the second biggest trade partner of India.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/India-Afghanistan_relations" class="mw-redirect" title="India-Afghanistan relations">Relations with Afghanistan</a> have improved considerably, with India now becoming the largest regional donor to Afghanistan.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> During Afghan President <a href="/wiki/Hamid_Karzai" title="Hamid Karzai">Hamid Karzai</a>'s visit to New Delhi in August 2008, Manmohan Singh increased the aid package to Afghanistan for the development of more schools, health clinics, infrastructure, and defence.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC2_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC2-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Under the leadership of Singh, India emerged as one of the single largest aid donors to Afghanistan.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC2_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC2-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Dmitry_Medvedev_in_China_14_April_2011-2.jpeg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Dmitry_Medvedev_in_China_14_April_2011-2.jpeg/220px-Dmitry_Medvedev_in_China_14_April_2011-2.jpeg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Dmitry_Medvedev_in_China_14_April_2011-2.jpeg/330px-Dmitry_Medvedev_in_China_14_April_2011-2.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Dmitry_Medvedev_in_China_14_April_2011-2.jpeg/440px-Dmitry_Medvedev_in_China_14_April_2011-2.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="650" data-file-height="434" /></a><figcaption>Prime Minister Manmohan Singh with <a href="/wiki/Dmitry_Medvedev" title="Dmitry Medvedev">Dmitry Medvedev</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hu_Jintao" title="Hu Jintao">Hu Jintao</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dilma_Rousseff" title="Dilma Rousseff">Dilma Rousseff</a> and <a href="/wiki/Jacob_Zuma" title="Jacob Zuma">Jacob Zuma</a> at the <a href="/wiki/2011_BRICS_summit" class="mw-redirect" title="2011 BRICS summit">2011 BRICS summit</a> in <a href="/wiki/Sanya" title="Sanya">Sanya</a>, China</figcaption></figure>
<p>Singh's government worked towards stronger <a href="/wiki/India-US_relations" class="mw-redirect" title="India-US relations">ties with the United States</a>. He visited the United States in July 2005 initiating negotiations over the <a href="/wiki/Indo-US_civilian_nuclear_agreement" class="mw-redirect" title="Indo-US civilian nuclear agreement">Indo-US civilian nuclear agreement</a>. This was followed by <a href="/wiki/George_W._Bush" title="George W. Bush">George W. Bush</a>'s successful visit to India in March 2006, during which the declaration over the nuclear agreement was made, giving India access to American nuclear fuel and technology while India will have to allow <a href="/wiki/IAEA" class="mw-redirect" title="IAEA">IAEA</a> inspection of its civil <a href="/wiki/Nuclear_reactor" title="Nuclear reactor">nuclear reactors</a>. After more than two years for more negotiations, followed by approval from the IAEA, <a href="/wiki/Nuclear_Suppliers_Group" title="Nuclear Suppliers Group">Nuclear Suppliers Group</a> and the <a href="/wiki/US_Congress" class="mw-redirect" title="US Congress">US Congress</a>, India and the US signed the agreement on 10 October 2008 with <a href="/wiki/Pranab_Mukherjee" title="Pranab Mukherjee">Pranab Mukherjee</a> representing India.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Singh had the first official state visit to the White House during the administration of US President <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Barack Obama</a>. The visit took place in November 2009, and several discussions took place, including on trade and nuclear power.<sup id="cite_ref-CSIS_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CSIS-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Relations have improved with Japan and <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a> countries, like the United Kingdom, France, and Germany. Relations with Iran have continued and negotiations over the <a href="/wiki/Iran-Pakistan-India_gas_pipeline" class="mw-redirect" title="Iran-Pakistan-India gas pipeline">Iran-Pakistan-India gas pipeline</a> have taken place. New Delhi hosted an India–Africa Summit in April 2006 which was attended by the leaders of 15 African states.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Relations have improved with other developing countries, particularly Brazil and South Africa. Singh carried forward the momentum which was established after the "Brasilia Declaration" in 2003 and the <a href="/wiki/IBSA_Dialogue_Forum" title="IBSA Dialogue Forum">IBSA Dialogue Forum</a> was formed.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Singh's government has also been especially keen on <a href="/wiki/India-Israel_relations" class="mw-redirect" title="India-Israel relations">expanding ties with Israel</a>. Since 2003, the two countries have made significant investments in each other<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> and Israel now rivals Russia to become India's defence partner.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Though there have been a few diplomatic glitches between India and Russia, especially over the delay and price hike of several Russian weapons to be delivered to India,<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> relations between the two remain strong with India and Russia signing various agreements to increase defence, nuclear energy and space co-operation.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Second_term:_2009.E2.80.932014"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_term:_2009–2014">Second term: 2009–2014</span></h3>
<p>India held general elections to the 15th Lok Sabha in five phases between 16 April 2009 and 13 May 2009. The results of the election were announced on 16 May 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
Strong showing in <a href="/wiki/Andhra_Pradesh" title="Andhra Pradesh">Andhra Pradesh</a>, Rajasthan, <a href="/wiki/Maharashtra" title="Maharashtra">Maharashtra</a>, Tamil Nadu, <a href="/wiki/Kerala" title="Kerala">Kerala</a>, West Bengal and <a href="/wiki/Uttar_Pradesh" title="Uttar Pradesh">Uttar Pradesh</a> helped the <a href="/wiki/United_Progressive_Alliance" title="United Progressive Alliance">United Progressive Alliance</a> (UPA) form the new government under the incumbent Singh, who became the first prime minister since <a href="/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru" title="Jawaharlal Nehru">Jawaharlal Nehru</a> in <a href="/wiki/1962_Indian_general_election" title="1962 Indian general election">1962</a> to win re-election after completing a full five-year term.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> The Congress and its allies were able to put together a comfortable majority with support from 322 members out of 543 members of the House. These included those of the UPA and the external support from the <a href="/wiki/Bahujan_Samaj_Party" title="Bahujan Samaj Party">Bahujan Samaj Party</a> (BSP), <a href="/wiki/Samajwadi_Party" title="Samajwadi Party">Samajwadi Party</a> (SP), <a href="/wiki/Janata_Dal_(Secular)" title="Janata Dal (Secular)">Janata Dal (Secular)</a> (JD(S)), <a href="/wiki/Rashtriya_Janata_Dal" title="Rashtriya Janata Dal">Rashtriya Janata Dal</a> (RJD) and other minor parties.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 22 May 2009, Manmohan Singh was sworn in as the Prime Minister during a ceremony held at <a href="/wiki/Rashtrapati_Bhavan" title="Rashtrapati Bhavan">Rashtrapati Bhavan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/2009_Indian_general_election" title="2009 Indian general election">2009 Indian general election</a> was the largest democratic election in the world held prior to 2014 (834 million) and 2019 (912 million), with an eligible electorate of 714 million.
</p><p>The 2012 report filed by the CAG in Parliament of India states that due to the allocation of coal blocks to certain private companies without bidding process the nation suffered an estimated loss of Rs 1.85&#160;trillion (<a href="/wiki/Long_and_short_scales" title="Long and short scales">short scale</a>) between 2005 and 2009 in which Manmohan Singh was the coal minister of India.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Manmohan Singh declined to appear before a <a href="/wiki/Joint_parliamentary_committee" title="Joint parliamentary committee">Joint Parliamentary Committee</a> (JPC) in April 2013 when called upon by one of the members of JPC <a href="/wiki/Yashwant_Sinha" title="Yashwant Sinha">Yashwant Sinha</a> for his alleged involvement in the 2G case.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Post-premiership_.282014.E2.80.93present.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-premiership_(2014–present)">Post-premiership (2014–present)</span></h2>
<p>Singh's premiership officially ended at noon on 17 May 2014. He did not contest the <a href="/wiki/2014_Indian_general_election" title="2014 Indian general election">2014 general election</a> for the 16th Lok Sabha as the prime ministeral candidate. Singh resigned his post as prime minister, after the Bharatiya Janata Party led National Democratic Alliance won the 2014 Lok Sabha election. He served as the acting prime minister till 25 May 2014, when <a href="/wiki/Narendra_Modi" title="Narendra Modi">Narendra Modi</a> was sworn in as the new prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Singh along with Congress president <a href="/wiki/Sonia_Gandhi" title="Sonia Gandhi">Sonia Gandhi</a>, former Presidents <a href="/wiki/A._P._J._Abdul_Kalam" title="A. P. J. Abdul Kalam">A. P. J. Abdul Kalam</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pratibha_Patil" title="Pratibha Patil">Pratibha Patil</a>, Vice President <a href="/wiki/Hamid_Ansari" class="mw-redirect" title="Hamid Ansari">Hamid Ansari</a> attended Narendra Modi's <a href="/wiki/First_swearing-in_ceremony_of_Narendra_Modi" title="First swearing-in ceremony of Narendra Modi">swearing-in ceremony</a>. After the swearing-in ceremony Singh shifted to 3 Motilal Nehru Road, New Delhi. In 2016 it was announced that Singh was to take up a position at <a href="/wiki/Panjab_University" title="Panjab University">Panjab University</a> as the Jawaharlal Nehru Chair, which he eventually never did.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Public_image">Public image</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Prime_Minister_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_meeting_with_the_Nobel_Peace_Prize_winner_Dr._Mohammed_Yunus,_in_New_Delhi_on_January_30,_2007.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Manmohan Singh and Muhammad Yunus meet in India" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/The_Prime_Minister_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_meeting_with_the_Nobel_Peace_Prize_winner_Dr._Mohammed_Yunus%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_January_30%2C_2007.jpg/250px-The_Prime_Minister_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_meeting_with_the_Nobel_Peace_Prize_winner_Dr._Mohammed_Yunus%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_January_30%2C_2007.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="162" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/The_Prime_Minister_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_meeting_with_the_Nobel_Peace_Prize_winner_Dr._Mohammed_Yunus%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_January_30%2C_2007.jpg/375px-The_Prime_Minister_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_meeting_with_the_Nobel_Peace_Prize_winner_Dr._Mohammed_Yunus%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_January_30%2C_2007.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/The_Prime_Minister_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_meeting_with_the_Nobel_Peace_Prize_winner_Dr._Mohammed_Yunus%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_January_30%2C_2007.jpg/500px-The_Prime_Minister_Dr._Manmohan_Singh_meeting_with_the_Nobel_Peace_Prize_winner_Dr._Mohammed_Yunus%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_January_30%2C_2007.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2100" data-file-height="1361" /></a><figcaption>Singh meeting with <a href="/wiki/Nobel_Peace_Prize" title="Nobel Peace Prize">Nobel Peace Prize</a> winner <a href="/wiki/Muhammad_Yunus" title="Muhammad Yunus">Muhammad Yunus</a></figcaption></figure>
<p><i><a href="/wiki/The_Independent" title="The Independent">The Independent</a></i> described Singh as "one of the world's most revered leaders" and "a man of uncommon decency and grace", noting that he drives a <a href="/wiki/Maruti_800" title="Maruti 800">Maruti 800</a>, one of the humblest cars in the Indian market. <a href="/wiki/Khushwant_Singh" title="Khushwant Singh">Khushwant Singh</a> lauded Singh as the best prime minister India has had, even rating him higher than <a href="/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru" title="Jawaharlal Nehru">Jawaharlal Nehru</a>. He mentions an incident in his book <i>Absolute Khushwant: The Low-Down on Life, Death and Most things In-between</i> where after losing the 1999 Lok Sabha elections, Singh immediately returned the <span class="nowrap"><span style="white-space: nowrap">₹</span>2 lakh</span> (US$2,500) he had borrowed from the writer for hiring taxis. Terming him as the best example of integrity, Khushwant Singh stated, "When people talk of integrity, I say the best example is the man who occupies the country's highest office."<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2010, <a href="/wiki/Newsweek" title="Newsweek"><i>Newsweek</i> magazine</a> recognised him as a world leader who is respected by other heads of state, describing him as "the leader other leaders love." The article quoted <a href="/wiki/Mohamed_ElBaradei" title="Mohamed ElBaradei">Mohamed ElBaradei</a>, who remarked that Singh is "the model of what a political leader should be."<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> Singh also received the World Statesman Award in 2010. <a href="/wiki/Henry_Kissinger" title="Henry Kissinger">Henry Kissinger</a> described Singh as "a statesman with vision, persistence and integrity", and praised him for his "leadership, which has been instrumental in the economic transformation underway in India."<sup id="cite_ref-worldstatesmanaward_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-worldstatesmanaward-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Manmohan Singh was ranked 18 on the 2010 <a href="/wiki/Forbes_list_of_the_World%27s_Most_Powerful_People" title="Forbes list of the World&#39;s Most Powerful People">Forbes list of the World's Most Powerful People</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> <i><a href="/wiki/Forbes" title="Forbes">Forbes</a></i> magazine described Singh as being "universally praised as India's best prime minister since Nehru".<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Australian journalist <a href="/wiki/Greg_Sheridan" title="Greg Sheridan">Greg Sheridan</a> praised Singh "as one of the greatest statesmen in Asian history."<sup id="cite_ref-TA_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TA-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Singh was later ranked 19 and 28 in 2012 and 2013 in the Forbes list.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Stamps_of_Uzbekistan,_2006-055.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Postal stamp to honor Manmohan Singh" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Stamps_of_Uzbekistan%2C_2006-055.jpg/220px-Stamps_of_Uzbekistan%2C_2006-055.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="158" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Stamps_of_Uzbekistan%2C_2006-055.jpg/330px-Stamps_of_Uzbekistan%2C_2006-055.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Stamps_of_Uzbekistan%2C_2006-055.jpg/440px-Stamps_of_Uzbekistan%2C_2006-055.jpg 2x" data-file-width="620" data-file-height="445" /></a><figcaption>Stamp launched in <a href="/wiki/Uzbekistan" title="Uzbekistan">Uzbekistan</a> in honor of Manmohan Singh in 2006</figcaption></figure>
<p>Conversely, <i><a href="/wiki/Time_(magazine)" title="Time (magazine)">Time</a></i> magazine's Asia edition for 10–17 July 2012, on its cover remarked that Singh was an "underachiever".<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> It stated that Singh appears "unwilling to stick his neck out" on reforms that will put the country back onto a growth path. Congress spokesperson <a href="/wiki/Manish_Tewari" title="Manish Tewari">Manish Tewari</a> rebutted the charges. UPA ally <a href="/wiki/Lalu_Prasad_Yadav" title="Lalu Prasad Yadav">Lalu Prasad Yadav</a> took issue with the magazine's statements. Praising the government, Prasad said UPA projects [were] doing well and asked, "What will America say as their own economy is shattered?".<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Political opponents, including BJP co-founder <a href="/wiki/L._K._Advani" title="L. K. Advani">L. K. Advani</a>, have claimed that Singh is a "weak" prime minister. Advani declared "He is weak. What do I call a person who can't take his decisions until <a href="/wiki/10_Janpath" class="mw-redirect" title="10 Janpath">10 Janpath</a> gives instruction."<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> In 2013, BJP leader <a href="/wiki/Narendra_Modi" title="Narendra Modi">Narendra Modi</a> had termed Singh as "night watchman" and a "puppet of the Gandhi family".<sup id="cite_ref-NDTV.com_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NDTV.com-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the recent years, political opponents have praised Singh. <a href="/wiki/Arvind_Kejriwal" title="Arvind Kejriwal">Arvind Kejriwal</a> in 2018 said the people are "missing an educated PM like Dr Manmohan Singh".<sup id="cite_ref-NDTV.com2_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NDTV.com2-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> In 2022, <a href="/wiki/Nitin_Gadkari" title="Nitin Gadkari">Nitin Gadkari</a> said the "country is indebted to Manmohan Singh for the liberalisation that gave a new direction".<sup id="cite_ref-NDTV.com3_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NDTV.com3-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Degrees_and_posts_held">Degrees and posts held</span></h2>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Arts" title="Bachelor of Arts">B.A</a> (Honours) in <a href="/wiki/Economics" title="Economics">Economics</a> 1952; <a href="/wiki/Master_of_Arts" title="Master of Arts">M.A</a> (<a href="/wiki/Academic_grading_in_India" title="Academic grading in India">First Class</a>) in Economics, 1954 <a href="/wiki/Panjab_University,_Chandigarh" class="mw-redirect" title="Panjab University, Chandigarh">Panjab University, Chandigarh</a> (then in <a href="/wiki/Hoshiarpur" title="Hoshiarpur">Hoshiarpur</a>, Punjab), India</li>
<li>Honours degree in Economics, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Cambridge" title="University of Cambridge">University of Cambridge</a> – <a href="/wiki/St_John%27s_College,_Cambridge" title="St John&#39;s College, Cambridge">St John's College</a> (1957)
<ul><li>Senior Lecturer, Economics (1957–1959)</li>
<li>Reader (1959–1963)</li>
<li>Professor (1963–1965)</li>
<li>Professor of International Trade (1969–1971)</li></ul></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_Philosophy" title="Doctor of Philosophy">DPhil</a> in Economics, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Oxford" title="University of Oxford">University of Oxford</a> – <a href="/wiki/Nuffield_College,_Oxford" title="Nuffield College, Oxford">Nuffield College</a> (1962)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Delhi_School_of_Economics" title="Delhi School of Economics">Delhi School of Economics</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Delhi" class="mw-redirect" title="University of Delhi">University of Delhi</a>
<ul><li>Honorary Professor (1966)</li></ul></li>
<li>Chief, Financing for Trade Section, UNCTAD, United Nations Secretariat, New York
<ul><li>1966&#160;: Economic Affairs Officer 1966</li></ul></li>
<li>Economic Adviser, Ministry of Foreign Trade, India (1971–1972)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Chief_Economic_Advisor_to_the_Government_of_India" title="Chief Economic Advisor to the Government of India">Chief Economic Adviser</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Finance_(India)" title="Ministry of Finance (India)">Ministry of Finance</a>, India, (1972–1976)</li>
<li>Honorary Professor, <a href="/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru_University,_Delhi" class="mw-redirect" title="Jawaharlal Nehru University, Delhi">Jawaharlal Nehru University</a>, New Delhi (1976)</li>
<li>Director, <a href="/wiki/Reserve_Bank_of_India" title="Reserve Bank of India">Reserve Bank of India</a> (1976–1980)</li>
<li>Director, <a href="/wiki/Industrial_Development_Bank_of_India" class="mw-redirect" title="Industrial Development Bank of India">Industrial Development Bank of India</a> (1976–1980)</li>
<li>Board of Governors, <a href="/wiki/Asian_Development_Bank" title="Asian Development Bank">Asian Development Bank</a>, Manila</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Economic_Affairs_Secretary" class="mw-redirect" title="Economic Affairs Secretary">Secretary</a>, Ministry of Finance (Department of Economic Affairs), <a href="/wiki/Government_of_India" title="Government of India">Government of India</a>, (1977–1980)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Governor_of_Reserve_Bank_of_India" class="mw-redirect" title="Governor of Reserve Bank of India">Governor</a>, Reserve Bank of India (1982–1985)</li>
<li>Deputy chairman, <a href="/wiki/Planning_Commission_(India)" title="Planning Commission (India)">Planning Commission of India</a>, (1985–1987)</li>
<li>Secretary General, South Commission, Geneva (1987–1990)</li>
<li>Advisor to <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_India" title="Prime Minister of India">Prime Minister of India</a> on Economic Affairs (1990–1991)</li>
<li>Chairman, <a href="/wiki/University_Grants_Commission_(India)" title="University Grants Commission (India)">University Grants Commission</a> (15 March 1991 – 20 June 1991)<sup id="cite_ref-dr_mms_4-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dr_mms-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_(India)" title="Minister of Finance (India)">Finance Minister of India</a>, (21 June 1991 – 15 May 1996)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament,_Rajya_Sabha" title="Member of Parliament, Rajya Sabha">Member of Parliament</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Rajya_Sabha" title="Rajya Sabha">Rajya Sabha</a> (1 October 1991 – 14 June 2019)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(India)" title="Leader of the Opposition (India)">Leader of the Opposition (India)</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Rajya_Sabha" title="Rajya Sabha">Rajya Sabha</a> (1998–2004)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_India" title="Prime Minister of India">Prime Minister of India</a> (22 May 2004&#160;– 26 May 2014)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament,_Rajya_Sabha" title="Member of Parliament, Rajya Sabha">Member of Parliament</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Rajya_Sabha" title="Rajya Sabha">Rajya Sabha</a> (19 August 2019 – Present)</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_popular_culture">In popular culture</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Category:Cultural_depictions_of_Manmohan_Singh" title="Category:Cultural depictions of Manmohan Singh">Category:Cultural depictions of Manmohan Singh</a></div>
<p>A Bollywood film was made in 2019 based on Singh's life,  titled <i><a href="/wiki/The_Accidental_Prime_Minister_(film)" title="The Accidental Prime Minister (film)">The Accidental Prime Minister</a></i> directed by <a href="/wiki/Vijay_Gutte" title="Vijay Gutte">Vijay Gutte</a> and written by Mayank Tewari.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> The film was based on the 2014 memoir of the <a href="/wiki/The_Accidental_Prime_Minister" title="The Accidental Prime Minister">same name</a> by <a href="/wiki/Sanjaya_Baru" title="Sanjaya Baru">Sanjaya Baru</a> with <a href="/wiki/Anupam_Kher" title="Anupam Kher">Anupam Kher</a> in the titular role.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><span title="Hindi-language text"><i lang="hi-Latn"><a href="/wiki/Pradhanmantri" title="Pradhanmantri">Pradhanmantri</a></i></span> (<abbr style="font-size:85%" title="literal translation">lit.</abbr><span style="white-space: nowrap;">&#8201;</span>'Prime Minister&#39;), a 2013 Indian documentary television series which aired on <a href="/wiki/ABP_News" title="ABP News">ABP News</a> and covers the various policies and political tenures of Indian PMs, includes the tenureship of Manmohan Singh in the episodes "Story of Sonia Gandhi and UPA-I Government", and "Scams in UPA government and anti-corruption movement".<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
